How to read this report
  • A run's score is the weighted fraction of its graders that passed; a "perfect" run passed every grader.
  • A case's score is the mean of its runs; a case passes when its score is at or above the 100% threshold (the tick on each case bar).
  • The suite score is the mean of the per-case scores.
  • Judge votes are independent samples of the LLM judge; the majority decides pass or fail.
  • "With plugin" and "Baseline" runs are identical except for the plugin being loaded; Δ is the with-plugin score minus the baseline score.

trg-07-l1-no-corner

evals/tune-regularization/trg-07-l1-no-corner ↑ +24.6 pts with plugin 100%
Results
With plugin 100% 100% of runs perfect
Run 1 100% 7 turns · $3.25 · 20:21:08 UTC
✓ pass criterion

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I recommend **`eps ≈ 0.55`**, chosen by the discrepancy principle. The L-curve has no clear corner, so I did not use it. `sweep.py` is saved and runs, and its last line is `EPS_CHOSEN=0.5489`. The plot is `sweep.png`.

**What I ran:** 60 `fista` solves with `eps` log-spaced from 4.18 down to 4.2e-4, which is 4 decades below the value where the model is all zeros. Each solve used 3000 iterations, a fixed step `alpha` and a warm start from the previous solution.

| Criterion | eps | Non-zeros | Residual | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| L-curve max curvature | 1.4 | 6 | 2.06 | 5.5 dB |
| Discrepancy, residual ≤ σ√M = 1.34 | **0.55** | 17 | 1.27 | 11.2 dB |
| Best SNR (needs `xtrue`) | 0.25 | 42 | – | 12.3 dB |

**Why not the L-curve:**
- In the log-log plot the curve is a smooth bend from a flat branch into a steep one. The curvature peak is only about 5 times the median curvature.
- The max-curvature point at `eps=1.4` has a residual of 2.06, about 1.5 times the noise level of 1.34. It sits on the steep branch, where the L1 penalty is shrinking the amplitudes, so the fit is biased.
- Its SNR is 5.5 dB, against 12.3 dB at the best value. I would not defend that number.
- This is common with L1 terms and noisy data, so I am reporting it as "no clear corner" rather than forcing one.

**Why the discrepancy principle:** you know σ = 0.15, so it gives a value tied to the data. It picks the largest `eps` whose residual still matches the noise. It recovers 11.2 dB, close to the best possible 12.3 dB.

**Things to know:**
- At `eps=0.55` the model has 17 non-zeros, not the 6 true ones. They are the 6 large spikes plus small noise-fitting entries. The 17 is only the count above a 1e-6 cutoff, so I did not check how many of those entries are small.
- At your `eps=2.0` the model is over-regularised: 4 to 5 non-zeros, a residual of about 2, and shrunken amplitudes.
- To get closer to 6 non-zeros you can raise `eps` toward 1. That trades SNR for sparsity, so decide based on what you need the model for.

**Change in your script:** `fista(Op, y, eps=0.55, niter=500)`. Note that 500 iterations may be too few at small `eps`, and I used 3000. For a final run, check that the cost has stopped changing.
✓ pass final-value ×0.5

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` (for `lsqr` only with `x0=None`, see step 3) |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`. **Exception:\n  `damp` of `lsqr`** (PyLops and SciPy): with `x0` it solves for the correction\n  `x - x0`, so it penalises `damp² ‖x - x0‖²`, not `damp² ‖x‖²`, and a\n  warm-started sweep is biased (residual too low, discrepancy pick too large).\n  Use `x0=None` for every `lsqr(damp=...)` solve, or put the penalty in the\n  operator (`VStack([Op, eps*Reg])`, `regularized_inversion`), where `x0` is\n  safe. `cgls(damp=...)` and `epsRs` are not affected (checked numerically);\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. Hand off\n\nReport:\n\n- the parameter swept, the range and the number of solves;\n- the value picked by each criterion and the one recommended, with the reason;\n- the plot file(s) (L-curve, residual vs ε, SNR vs ε if available) and the\n  model at the chosen value next to one over- and one under-regularised model;\n- the exact line to change in the user's inversion script.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"dcffca31-e295-4501-a6c4-4862b24b9718","uuid":"c1ea10b6-5b24-49b1-b578-90aba00011d6","timestamp":"2026-11-01T20:21:11.145Z","isSynthetic":true}

import numpy as np, pylops
import matplotlib; matplotlib.use("Agg")
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
N, M, sigma = 200, 80, 0.15
A = rng.standard_normal((M, N)) / np.sqrt(M)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
xtrue = np.zeros(N)
xtrue[rng.choice(N, 6, replace=False)] = rng.uniform(1, 3, 6) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 6)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(M)

niter = 3000
alpha = 1.0 / (np.abs(np.linalg.eigvalsh(A.T @ A)).max() * 1.0001)  # fixed step -> same for all solves
def solve(eps, x0):
    return pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(Op, y, x0=x0, eps=eps, niter=niter,
                                              alpha=alpha, tol=1e-12)[0]

eps0 = 2 * np.abs(A.T @ y).max() / (2 * alpha) * alpha  # eps above which x=0: eps*alpha/2 >= alpha*max|A^T y|
eps0 = 2 * np.abs(A.T @ y).max()
epss = np.logspace(np.log10(eps0), np.log10(eps0) - 4, 60)  # large -> small, warm start
xs, res, l1, nnz, snrs = [], [], [], [], []
x0 = None
for e in epss:
    x = solve(e, x0); x0 = x
    xs.append(x); res.append(np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x)); l1.append(np.abs(x).sum())
    nnz.append(int((np.abs(x) > 1e-6).sum())); snrs.append(snr(xtrue, x))
res, l1, nnz, snrs = map(np.array, (res, l1, nnz, snrs))

# L-curve corner: max curvature of (log res, log l1), only where the model is nonzero
ok = l1 > 1e-8
lr, ll = np.log(res[ok]), np.log(l1[ok]); t = np.log(epss[ok])
d1 = np.gradient(lr, t); d2 = np.gradient(ll, t)
dd1 = np.gradient(d1, t); dd2 = np.gradient(d2, t)
curv = (d1 * dd2 - d2 * dd1) / (d1**2 + d2**2) ** 1.5
# keep only the proper orientation (convex corner) and smooth lightly
k = np.convolve(curv, np.ones(3) / 3, mode="same")
ic = np.argmax(np.abs(k)); eps_L = epss[ok][ic]
print(f"sweep: eps in [{epss.min():.3g}, {epss.max():.3g}], {len(epss)} solves, niter={niter}")
print(f"L-curve max-|curvature| eps = {eps_L:.4g} (nnz={nnz[ok][ic]}, res={res[ok][ic]:.3f})")

# discrepancy principle: largest eps with res <= tau*sigma*sqrt(M)
target = 1.0 * sigma * np.sqrt(M)
idx = np.where(res <= target)[0]
eps_D = epss[idx[0]] if len(idx) else np.nan
iD = idx[0] if len(idx) else None
print(f"noise target sigma*sqrt(M) = {target:.3f}")
print(f"discrepancy eps = {eps_D:.4g} (nnz={nnz[iD]}, res={res[iD]:.3f})")
ib = np.argmax(snrs)
print(f"best-SNR eps = {epss[ib]:.4g} (SNR={snrs[ib]:.2f} dB, nnz={nnz[ib]}); true nnz=6")
print(f"eps=2.0 for reference: nnz={nnz[np.argmin(abs(epss-2))]} at nearest grid point")

fig, ax = plt.subplots(1, 4, figsize=(18, 4))
ax[0].loglog(res[ok], l1[ok], ".-"); ax[0].plot(res[ok][ic], l1[ok][ic], "ro", label=f"corner eps={eps_L:.3g}")
ax[0].axvline(target, c="g", ls="--", label="sigma*sqrt(M)"); ax[0].set(xlabel="||y-Ax||", ylabel="||x||_1", title="L-curve"); ax[0].legend()
ax[1].loglog(epss, res); ax[1].axhline(target, c="g", ls="--"); ax[1].axvline(eps_L, c="r"); ax[1].set(xlabel="eps", title="residual")
ax[2].semilogx(epss, snrs); ax[2].axvline(eps_L, c="r"); ax[2].set(xlabel="eps", title="SNR (dB)")
ax[3].semilogx(epss, nnz); ax[3].axvline(eps_L, c="r"); ax[3].set(xlabel="eps", title="nonzeros")
plt.tight_layout(); plt.savefig("sweep.png", dpi=110)

# The L1 L-curve has no sharp corner (see sweep.png): the max-curvature point sits on the steep,
# shrinkage-dominated branch (residual well above the noise level). With sigma known, use discrepancy.
print(f"SNR at L-curve pick = {snrs[ok][ic]:.2f} dB, at discrepancy pick = {snrs[iD]:.2f} dB")
eps_chosen = eps_D
print("curvature peak sharpness (peak / median |k|): %.1f" % (abs(k[ic]) / np.median(abs(k))))
print(f"EPS_CHOSEN={eps_chosen:.4g}")

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` (for `lsqr` only with `x0=None`, see step 3) |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`. **Exception:\n  `damp` of `lsqr`** (PyLops and SciPy): with `x0` it solves for the correction\n  `x - x0`, so it penalises `damp² ‖x - x0‖²`, not `damp² ‖x‖²`, and a\n  warm-started sweep is biased (residual too low, discrepancy pick too large).\n  Use `x0=None` for every `lsqr(damp=...)` solve, or put the penalty in the\n  operator (`VStack([Op, eps*Reg])`, `regularized_inversion`), where `x0` is\n  safe. `cgls(damp=...)` and `epsRs` are not affected (checked numerically);\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I recommend `eps ≈ 0.56`, but that number comes from the discrepancy principle, not from an L-curve corner. The L-curve for this problem has no corner.

**What I ran:** `sweep.py` runs `fista` with `niter=3000` at 40 log-spaced values of `eps`, from about 100 down to 0.005. It warm-starts from large to small `eps`. It saves its plots to `sweep.png`.

**Why the L-curve fails:** in log-log, residual against L1 norm is a smooth curve with curvature below zero at every point (maximum −0.008). The script's raw "corner" was the last grid point, `eps=0.0035`, which fits the noise exactly with 80 non-zeros. I did not use it. The script now checks the curvature sign and says "NO corner" when it is never positive. I haven't looked at `sweep.png` itself, only the printed numbers.

**The criteria that do give an answer** (the true model has 6 non-zeros):

| Criterion | eps | residual | non-zeros | SNR |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Discrepancy (residual ≤ σ√M = 1.342) | **0.564** | 1.28 | 14 | 11.1 dB |
| Best SNR (uses the true model, so synthetic only) | 0.26 | 0.88 | 42 | 12.3 dB |
| Your `eps=2.0` | 2.0 | about 2.6 | 4–5 | low |

- **Your `eps=2.0` is over-regularised.** Its residual is well above the noise level of 1.34, which is why you only get 4 non-zeros and the amplitudes are shrunk. On the grid, `eps=2.59` has residual 2.98 and an SNR of only 2.2 dB.
- **Why discrepancy:** you know σ = 0.15, so the model should fit the data to about σ√M. That makes 0.56 a justified pick.
- **Best SNR picks a lower `eps`:** it lands at 0.26 with 42 non-zeros, which is far from sparse.
- **Expect more than 6 non-zeros at 0.56.** It leaves 14, because the L1 penalty at this level still admits small spurious entries. If you need exactly the 6 true spikes, take the recovered support and refit by least squares on it, or use `spgl1` with `sigma=1.34`.

**Change in your script:** `fista(Op, y, eps=0.56, niter=500)`. I used `niter=3000` in the sweep, so check that 500 iterations converge at this smaller `eps`.

The last line of the script's output is `EPS_CHOSEN=0.5644`. That is the nearest grid point, so it is not a finely tuned optimum.

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` (for `lsqr` only with `x0=None`, see step 3) |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`. **Exception:\n  `damp` of `lsqr`** (PyLops and SciPy): with `x0` it solves for the correction\n  `x - x0`, so it penalises `damp² ‖x - x0‖²`, not `damp² ‖x‖²`, and a\n  warm-started sweep is biased (residual too low, discrepancy pick too large).\n  Use `x0=None` for every `lsqr(damp=...)` solve, or put the penalty in the\n  operator (`VStack([Op, eps*Reg])`, `regularized_inversion`), where `x0` is\n  safe. `cgls(damp=...)` and `epsRs` are not affected (checked numerically);\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. To adapt it, replace ``build_problem`` and ``solve`` and keep\nthe sweep, the selection criteria and the plots.\n\nCriteria implemented:\n- L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log);\n- discrepancy principle (needs the noise standard deviation);\n- best SNR against a known model (synthetic tests only);\n- GCV, exact on dense matrices (small quadratic problems only).\n\"\"\"\n\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\n\nimport pylops\nfrom pylops.utils.metrics import snr\n\n\ndef build_problem(rng):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: return (Op, Reg, y, xtrue or None, noise_std or None).\"\"\"\n    nt = 201\n    t = np.arange(nt) * 0.004\n    h, _, hcenter = pylops.utils.wavelets.ricker(t[:41], f0=15)\n    Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h=h, offset=hcenter)\n    Reg = pylops.SecondDerivative(nt)\n\n    xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 3 * t) * np.exp(-t)  # smooth model\n    noise_std = 0.05\n    y = Op @ xtrue + noise_std * rng.standard_normal(nt)\n    return Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std\n\n\ndef solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: the user's solver call with everything but ``eps`` fixed.\"\"\"\n    Opaug = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * Reg])\n    yaug = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(Reg.shape[0])])\n    return pylops.cgls(Opaug, yaug, x0=x0, niter=200, tol=1e-10)[0]\n\n\ndef lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm):\n    \"\"\"Index of the L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log).\n\n    Points are ordered by increasing eps so that the corner between the\n    \"under-regularised\" (vertical) and \"over-regularised\" (horizontal) branches\n    has positive curvature. A second knee with opposite curvature can appear\n    at very large eps (model pushed into the null space of the regulariser):\n    it is not the L-curve corner.\n    \"\"\"\n    order = np.argsort(epsilons)\n    lr, lx = np.log(rnorm[order]), np.log(xnorm[order])\n    # parametrise by arc length to make finite differences uniform\n    s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(np.hypot(np.diff(lr), np.diff(lx)))])\n    dr, dx = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lx, s)\n    ddr, ddx = np.gradient(dr, s), np.gradient(dx, s)\n    kappa = (dr * ddx - ddr * dx) / (dr**2 + dx**2) ** 1.5\n    kappa[[0, -1]] = -np.inf  # ends are not valid corners\n    kappa_orig = np.empty_like(kappa)\n    kappa_orig[order] = kappa\n    return int(np.argmax(kappa_orig)), kappa_orig\n\n\ndef discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, n, tau=1.02):\n    \"\"\"Largest eps whose residual is below tau * noise level (None if none).\"\"\"\n    target = tau * noise_std * np.sqrt(n)\n    ok = np.where(rnorm <= target)[0]\n    return (int(ok[np.argmax(epsilons[ok])]) if ok.size else None), target\n\n\ndef gcv_dense(Op, Reg, y, epsilons):\n    \"\"\"Exact GCV for min ||y - H @ y\n        g.append(n * np.real(r.conj() @ r) / np.real(n - np.trace(H)) ** 2)\n    return int(np.argmin(g)), np.array(g)\n\n\ndef main(neps=25, seed=0, dense_gcv=True):\n    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)\n    Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std = build_problem(rng)\n\n    # log grid around a scale-based guess, solved from large to small eps\n    eps0 = np.linalg.norm(Op.H @ y) / np.linalg.norm(Reg.H @ (Reg @ (Op.H @ y)))\n    epsilons = eps0 * np.logspace(3, -3, neps)\n\n    xs, rnorm, xnorm = [], [], []\n    x0 = np.zeros(Op.shape[1], dtype=Op.dtype)\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        x = solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0)\n        x0 = x  # warm start\n        xs.append(x)\n        rnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(y - """L-curve / discrepancy / best-SNR sweep of eps for FISTA (sparse recovery)."""
import matplotlib
matplotlib.use("Agg")
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
N, M, SIGMA = 200, 80, 0.15
A = rng.standard_normal((M, N)) / np.sqrt(M)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
xtrue = np.zeros(N)
xtrue[rng.choice(N, 6, replace=False)] = rng.uniform(1, 3, 6) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 6)
y = Op @ xtrue + SIGMA * rng.standard_normal(M)

NITER = 3000
# eps*alpha/2 is the threshold; all-zero model when it exceeds max|Op^H y|
alpha = 1.0 / np.real(Op.eigs(neigs=1, symmetric=True)[0]) if False else 1.0 / np.linalg.norm(A, 2) ** 2
eps0 = 2 * np.max(np.abs(A.T @ y)) / alpha
epsilons = eps0 * np.logspace(0.3, -4, 40)  # large -> small, warm started

xs, rn, xn, nnz = [], [], [], []
x0 = np.zeros(N)
for eps in epsilons:
    x = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(Op, y, x0=x0, eps=eps, niter=NITER, tol=1e-10, show=False)[0]
    x0 = x
    xs.append(x); rn.append(np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x)); xn.append(np.abs(x).sum())
    nnz.append(int(np.sum(np.abs(x) > 1e-6)))
rn, xn, nnz = map(np.array, (rn, xn, nnz))
snrs = np.array([snr(xtrue, x) for x in xs])

# L-curve corner: max curvature in log-log, ordered by increasing eps
valid = (xn > 0) & (rn > 0)
order = np.argsort(epsilons)
order = order[valid[order]]
lr, lx = np.log(rn[order]), np.log(xn[order])
s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(np.hypot(np.diff(lr), np.diff(lx)))])
dr, dx = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lx, s)
ddr, ddx = np.gradient(dr, s), np.gradient(dx, s)
kappa = (dr * ddx - ddr * dx) / (dr**2 + dx**2) ** 1.5
kappa[[0, -1]] = -np.inf
icorner = int(order[np.argmax(kappa)])
print("curvature max=%.2f, 2nd largest=%.2f" % (np.sort(kappa)[-1], np.sort(kappa)[-2]))

target = 1.0 * SIGMA * np.sqrt(M)
ok = np.where(rn <= target)[0]
idisc = int(ok[np.argmax(epsilons[ok])]) if ok.size else None
ibest = int(np.argmax(snrs))
has_corner = np.max(kappa[1:-1]) > 0
if not has_corner:
    print("L-curve: curvature never positive -> NO corner (L1 curve is smooth); ignoring it")
picks = {"best SNR": ibest}
if has_corner:
    picks["L-curve"] = icorner
if idisc is not None:
    picks["discrepancy"] = idisc
if has_corner:
    ichosen = icorner
else:
    ichosen = idisc  # discrepancy principle (tau=1) on the known sigma
for k, i in picks.items():
    print(f"{k:12s}: eps={epsilons[i]:.3e} ||r||={rn[i]:.3f} L1={xn[i]:.2f} nnz={nnz[i]} SNR={snrs[i]:.1f} dB")
print(f"noise level sigma*sqrt(M) = {target:.3f}; eps=2.0 -> nnz at nearest grid:",
      nnz[np.argmin(abs(epsilons - 2.0))])

fig, axs = plt.subplots(1, 4, figsize=(19, 4))
axs[0].loglog(rn, xn, ".-k")
for k, i in picks.items():
    axs[0].loglog(rn[i], xn[i], "o", label=k)
axs[0].set(xlabel="||y-Op x||", ylabel="||x||_1", title="L-curve"); axs[0].legend()
axs[1].loglog(epsilons, rn, ".-k"); axs[1].axhline(target, color="r", ls="--")
axs[1].set(xlabel="eps", ylabel="residual", title="residual vs eps")
a2 = axs[1].twinx(); a2.semilogx(epsilons, snrs, ".-b"); a2.set_ylabel("SNR dB", color="b")
axs[2].semilogx(epsilons, nnz, ".-"); axs[2].set(xlabel="eps", title="non-zeros")
axs[3].plot(xtrue, "k", lw=2, label="true")
for nm, i in {"over": min(icorner + 8, len(epsilons) - 1) if False else int(np.argmin(abs(epsilons - 5 * epsilons[ichosen]))),
              "chosen": ichosen,
              "under": int(np.argmin(abs(epsilons - epsilons[ichosen] / 30)))}.items():
    axs[3].plot(xs[i], label=f"{nm} eps={epsilons[i]:.2g}", alpha=.7)
axs[3].legend(); fig.tight_layout(); fig.savefig("sweep.png", dpi=110)

chosen = epsilons[ichosen]
print(f"EPS_CHOSEN={chosen:.4g}")

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` (for `lsqr` only with `x0=None`, see step 3) |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`. **Exception:\n  `damp` of `lsqr`** (PyLops and SciPy): with `x0` it solves for the correction\n  `x - x0`, so it penalises `damp² ‖x - x0‖²`, not `damp² ‖x‖²`, and a\n  warm-started sweep is biased (residual too low, discrepancy pick too large).\n  Use `x0=None` for every `lsqr(damp=...)` solve, or put the penalty in the\n  operator (`VStack([Op, eps*Reg])`, `regularized_inversion`), where `x0` is\n  safe. `cgls(damp=...)` and `epsRs` are not affected (checked numerically);\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. Hand off\n\nReport:\n\n- the parameter swept, the range and the number of solves;\n- the value picked by each criterion and the one recommended, with the reason;\n- the plot file(s) (L-curve, residual vs ε, SNR vs ε if available) and the\n  model at the chosen value next to one over- and one under-regularised model;\n- the exact line to change in the user's inversion script.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"834b25b2-5cdc-4547-bc97-9080eab59586","uuid":"6e210ef7-17a6-4f2b-8d20-2e6997205fd8","timestamp":"2026-11-01T20:21:40.174Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01WYidyTeQ2vNw7rTBHrnU9A","type":"tool_result","content":"lcurve_template.py\nregularization_sweep.png\n\"\"\"Template for choosing a regularisation weight in a PyLops inversion.\n\nRuns as-is on a toy 1-D deconvolution with second-derivative (smoothness)\nregularisation. To adapt it, replace ``build_problem`` and ``solve`` and keep\nthe sweep, the selection criteria and the plots.\n\nCriteria implemented:\n- L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log);\n- discrepancy principle (needs the noise standard deviation);\n- best SNR against a known model (synthetic tests only);\n- GCV, exact on dense matrices (small quadratic problems only).\n\"\"\"\n\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\n\nimport pylops\nfrom pylops.utils.metrics import snr\n\n\ndef build_problem(rng):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: return (Op, Reg, y, xtrue or None, noise_std or None).\"\"\"\n    nt = 201\n    t = np.arange(nt) * 0.004\n    h, _, hcenter = pylops.utils.wavelets.ricker(t[:41], f0=15)\n    Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h=h, offset=hcenter)\n    Reg = pylops.SecondDerivative(nt)\n\n    xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 3 * t) * np.exp(-t)  # smooth model\n    noise_std = 0.05\n    y = Op @ xtrue + noise_std * rng.standard_normal(nt)\n    return Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std\n\n\ndef solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: the user's solver call with everything but ``eps`` fixed.\"\"\"\n    Opaug = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * Reg])\n    yaug = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(Reg.shape[0])])\n    return pylops.cgls(Opaug, yaug, x0=x0, niter=200, tol=1e-10)[0]\n\n\ndef lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm):\n    \"\"\"Index of the L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log).\n\n    Points are ordered by increasing eps so that the corner between the\n    \"under-regularised\" (vertical) and \"over-regularised\" (horizontal) branches\n    has positive curvature. A second knee with opposite curvature can appear\n    at very large eps (model pushed into the null space of the regulariser):\n    it is not the L-curve corner.\n    \"\"\"\n    order = np.argsort(epsilons)\n    lr, lx = np.log(rnorm[order]), np.log(xnorm[order])\n    # parametrise by arc length to make finite differences uniform\n    s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(np.hypot(np.diff(lr), np.diff(lx)))])\n    dr, dx = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lx, s)\n    ddr, ddx = np.gradient(dr, s), np.gradient(dx, s)\n    kappa = (dr * ddx - ddr * dx) / (dr**2 + dx**2) ** 1.5\n    kappa[[0, -1]] = -np.inf  # ends are not valid corners\n    kappa_orig = np.empty_like(kappa)\n    kappa_orig[order] = kappa\n    return int(np.argmax(kappa_orig)), kappa_orig\n\n\ndef discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, n, tau=1.02):\n    \"\"\"Largest eps whose residual is below tau * noise level (None if none).\"\"\"\n    target = tau * noise_std * np.sqrt(n)\n    ok = np.where(rnorm <= target)[0]\n    return (int(ok[np.argmax(epsilons[ok])]) if ok.size else None), target\n\n\ndef gcv_dense(Op, Reg, y, epsilons):\n    \"\"\"Exact GCV for min ||y - A x||^2 + eps^2 ||R x||^2 (small problems).\"\"\"\n    A, R = Op.todense(), Reg.todense()\n    AtA, RtR, n = A.T.conj() @ A, R.T.conj() @ R, A.shape[0]\n    g = []\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        H = A @ np.linalg.solve(AtA + eps**2 * RtR, A.T.conj())  # influence matrix\n        r = y - H @ y\n        g.append(n * np.real(r.conj() @ r) / np.real(n - np.trace(H)) ** 2)\n    return int(np.argmin(g)), np.array(g)\n\n\ndef main(neps=25, seed=0, dense_gcv=True):\n    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)\n    Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std = build_problem(rng)\n\n    # log grid around a scale-based guess, solved from large to small eps\n    eps0 = np.linalg.norm(Op.H @ y) / np.linalg.norm(Reg.H @ (Reg @ (Op.H @ y)))\n    epsilons = eps0 * np.logspace(3, -3, neps)\n\n    xs, rnorm, xnorm = [], [], []\n    x0 = np.zeros(Op.shape[1], dtype=Op.dtype)\n    for eps in epsilons:\n        x = solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0)\n        x0 = x  # warm start\n        xs.append(x)\n        rnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x))\n        xnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(Reg @ x))\n    rnorm, xnorm = np.array(rnorm), np.array(xnorm)\n\n    picks = {}\n    icorner, _ = lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm)\n    picks[\"L-curve\"] = icorner\n    if noise_std is not None:\n        idisc, target = discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, y.size)\n        if idisc is None:\n            print(f\"discrepancy: no eps reaches residual {target:.3e}; extend the grid\")\n        else:\n            picks[\"discrepancy\"] = idisc\n    snrs = None\n    if xtrue is not None:\n        snrs = np.array([snr(xtrue, x) for x in xs])\n        p

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I swept `eps` and the L-curve has no corner, so I can't give you a corner-based value. I recommend `eps ≈ 0.51` instead, from the discrepancy principle, and the script ends with `EPS_CHOSEN=0.511`.

**What I ran**
- `sweep.py` runs 40 `fista` solves on a log grid from 4e-4 to 8.3, with warm starts and `niter=3000` so each solve is converged.
- It makes the L-curve (‖y−Ax‖ against ‖x‖₁), the discrepancy pick and an SNR check against your known `xtrue`, and saves `sweep.png`.
- The grid top is about 2·max|Aᵀy|, which is where the model goes to all zeros.

**Why the L-curve gives no corner**
- The curvature is negative everywhere, with a maximum of about −0.001. The curve bends smoothly instead of showing an L.
- The automatic pick is `eps≈5e-4`, which fits the noise exactly (residual 0.003) and has 80 non-zeros. I rejected it rather than report it as the corner.
- This is common with L1 terms on noisy data. Here the problem is also underdetermined (80 data points, 200 unknowns).

**What the other criteria say**

| Criterion | `eps` | Non-zeros | SNR |
|---|---|---|---|
| Discrepancy (residual ≤ 0.15·√80 = 1.34) | **0.51** | 18 | 11.4 dB |
| Best SNR (needs the true model) | 0.24 | 43 | 12.3 dB |
| Your `eps=2.0` | 2.0 | 4–5 | about 3.5 dB |

**Why 0.51**
- It is the largest `eps` whose residual matches the known noise level, and it needs no knowledge of the true model.
- It is close to the best-SNR peak, within about 1 dB.
- At `eps=2.0` the residual is about 2.6, well above the noise level of 1.34. That model is over-regularised: it has too few spikes and a poor SNR.

**Caveats**
- 0.51 still gives 18 non-zeros against 6 true spikes. The L1 penalty adds small false spikes at the noise level before it recovers the full amplitude of the real ones.
- If you want about 6 non-zeros, `eps≈1.4` gives exactly 6 (SNR 5.4 dB), but the data are then under-fit. A common fix is to take the support from `fista` at `eps≈0.5–1.4` and re-fit by least squares on that support.
- I only tested one noise realisation.

**Line to change:** `eps=2.0` → `eps=0.51` in your `fista` call. I haven't looked at `sweep.png`, so check its three panels (L-curve, residual and SNR against `eps`, and the models at the chosen value and one on each side) before you rely on the pick.

PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` (for `lsqr` only with `x0=None`, see step 3) |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`. **Exception:\n  `damp` of `lsqr`** (PyLops and SciPy): with `x0` it solves for the correction\n  `x - x0`, so it penalises `damp² ‖x - x0‖²`, not `damp² ‖x‖²`, and a\n  warm-started sweep is biased (residual too low, discrepancy pick too large).\n  Use `x0=None` for every `lsqr(damp=...)` solve, or put the penalty in the\n  operator (`VStack([Op, eps*Reg])`, `regularized_inversion`), where `x0` is\n  safe. `cgls(damp=...)` and `epsRs` are not affected (checked numerically);\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. To adapt it, replace ``build_problem`` and ``solve`` and keep\nthe sweep, the selection criteria and the plots.\n\nCriteria implemented:\n- L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log);\n- discrepancy principle (needs the noise standard deviation);\n- best SNR against a known model (synthetic tests only);\n- GCV, exact on dense matrices (small quadratic problems only).\n\"\"\"\n\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\n\nimport pylops\nfrom pylops.utils.metrics import snr\n\n\ndef build_problem(rng):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: return (Op, Reg, y, xtrue or None, noise_std or None).\"\"\"\n    nt = 201\n    t = np.arange(nt) * 0.004\n    h, _, hcenter = pylops.utils.wavelets.ricker(t[:41], f0=15)\n    Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h=h, offset=hcenter)\n    Reg = pylops.SecondDerivative(nt)\n\n    xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 3 * t) * np.exp(-t)  # smooth model\n    noise_std = 0.05\n    y = Op @ xtrue + noise_std * rng.standard_normal(nt)\n    return Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std\n\n\ndef solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: the user's solver call with everything but ``eps`` fixed.\"\"\"\n    Opaug = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * Reg])\n    yaug = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(Reg.shape[0])])\n    return pylops.cgls(Opaug, yaug, x0=x0, niter=200, tol=1e-10)[0]\n\n\ndef lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm):\n    \"\"\"Index of the L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log).\n\n    Points are ordered by increasing eps so that the corner between the\n    \"under-regularised\" (vertical) and \"over-regularised\" (horizontal) branches\n    has positive curvature. """eps sweep for pylops fista: L-curve (||r|| vs ||x||_1), discrepancy, best SNR."""
import matplotlib
matplotlib.use("Agg")
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

NITER = 3000
SIGMA = 0.15

rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
N, M = 200, 80
A = rng.standard_normal((M, N)) / np.sqrt(M)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
xtrue = np.zeros(N)
xtrue[rng.choice(N, 6, replace=False)] = rng.uniform(1, 3, 6) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 6)
y = Op @ xtrue + SIGMA * rng.standard_normal(M)


def solve(eps, x0):
    return pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(Op, y, x0=x0, eps=eps, niter=NITER, tol=1e-12)[0]


def lcurve_corner(eps, r, xn):
    order = np.argsort(eps)
    lr, lx = np.log(r[order]), np.log(xn[order])
    s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(np.hypot(np.diff(lr), np.diff(lx)))])
    dr, dx = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lx, s)
    ddr, ddx = np.gradient(dr, s), np.gradient(dx, s)
    k = (dr * ddx - ddr * dx) / (dr**2 + dx**2) ** 1.5
    k[[0, -1]] = -np.inf
    ko = np.empty_like(k)
    ko[order] = k
    return int(np.argmax(ko)), ko


# all-zero model for eps >= 2 max|A^T y| (soft threshold eps*alpha/2 vs alpha*A^T y)
eps_max = 2 * np.abs(A.T @ y).max()
epsilons = eps_max * np.logspace(0.3, -4, 40)  # large -> small

xs, rn, xn, nnz = [], [], [], []
x0 = np.zeros(N)
for e in epsilons:
    x = solve(e, x0)
    x0 = x
    xs.append(x)
    rn.append(np.linalg.norm(y - A @ x))
    xn.append(np.abs(x).sum())
    nnz.append(int((np.abs(x) > 1e-8).sum()))
rn, xn, nnz = np.array(rn), np.array(xn), np.array(nnz)
snrs = np.array([snr(xtrue, x) for x in xs])

# use only the part of the curve with a non-trivial model for the corner
valid = xn > 0
ev, rv, xv = epsilons[valid], rn[valid], xn[valid]
ic_v, kappa = lcurve_corner(ev, rv, xv)
icorner = int(np.where(valid)[0][ic_v])

target = 1.0 * SIGMA * np.sqrt(M)
ok = np.where(rn <= target)[0]
idisc = int(ok[np.argmax(epsilons[ok])]) if ok.size else None
ibest = int(np.argmax(snrs))

has_corner = kappa[np.isfinite(kappa)].max() > 0.5  # no genuine knee if curvature never clearly positive
print("L-curve max curvature:", kappa[np.isfinite(kappa)].max(), "-> corner found" if has_corner else "-> NO clear corner")
picks = {"best SNR": ibest}
if has_corner:
    picks["L-curve"] = icorner
if idisc is not None:
    picks["discrepancy"] = idisc
print(f"swept eps in [{epsilons.min():.2e}, {epsilons.max():.2e}], {len(epsilons)} solves, niter={NITER}")
print(f"true nnz=6, noise level sigma*sqrt(M)={target:.3f}")
for n, i in picks.items():
    print(f"{n:12s}: eps={epsilons[i]:.3e} ||r||={rn[i]:.3f} ||x||_1={xn[i]:.2f} nnz={nnz[i]} SNR={snrs[i]:.2f} dB")
print(f"user's eps=2.0: nnz={(np.abs(solve(2.0, np.zeros(N)))>1e-8).sum()}")
print("kappa (valid pts):", np.round(kappa, 2))

fig, ax = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(15, 4))
ax[0].loglog(rn[valid], xn[valid], ".-k")
for n, i in picks.items():
    ax[0].loglog(rn[i], xn[i], "o", label=f"{n} (eps={epsilons[i]:.2g})")
ax[0].set_xlabel("||y-Ax||"); ax[0].set_ylabel("||x||_1"); ax[0].legend(); ax[0].set_title("L-curve")
ax[1].loglog(epsilons, rn, ".-k")
ax[1].axhline(target, color="r", ls="--", label="noise level")
a2 = ax[1].twinx(); a2.semilogx(epsilons, snrs, ".-b"); a2.set_ylabel("SNR dB", color="b")
ax[1].set_xlabel("eps"); ax[1].legend()
ax[2].plot(xtrue, "k", lw=2, label="true")
ichosen = icorner if has_corner else idisc
for nm, i in {"over": max(0, ichosen - 6), "chosen": ichosen, "under": min(len(epsilons)-1, ichosen + 8)}.items():
    ax[2].plot(xs[i], label=f"{nm} eps={epsilons[i]:.2g}", alpha=.7)
ax[2].legend()
fig.tight_layout(); fig.savefig("sweep.png", dpi=110)

# recommendation: L-curve corner is primary; fall back if no clear corner
for e, n_, s_, r_ in zip(epsilons, nnz, snrs, rn):
    print(f"  eps={e:.3e} nnz={n_:3d} SNR={s_:6.2f} ||r||={r_:.3f}")
print(f"EPS_CHOSEN={epsilons[ichosen]:.3g}")

PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` (for `lsqr` only with `x0=None`, see step 3) |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`. **Exception:\n  `damp` of `lsqr`** (PyLops and SciPy): with `x0` it solves for the correction\n  `x - x0`, so it penalises `damp² ‖x - x0‖²`, not `damp² ‖x‖²`, and a\n  warm-started sweep is biased (residual too low, discrepancy pick too large).\n  Use `x0=None` for every `lsqr(damp=...)` solve, or put the penalty in the\n  operator (`VStack([Op, eps*Reg])`, `regularized_inversion`), where `x0` is\n  safe. `cgls(damp=...)` and `epsRs` are not affected (checked numerically);\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01J7DngUmrE6qP1VNV9mv5Kg","type":"tool_result","content":"lcurve_template.py\nregularization_sweep.png\n\"\"\"Template for choosing a regularisation weight in a PyLops inversion.\n\nRuns as-is on a toy 1-D deconvolution with second-derivative (smoothness)\nregularisation. To adapt it, replace ``build_problem`` and ``solve`` and keep\nthe sweep, the selection criteria and the plots.\n\nCriteria implemented:\n- L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log);\n- discrepancy principle (needs the noise standard deviation);\n- best SNR against a known model (synthetic tests only);\n- GCV, exact on dense matrices (small quadratic problems only).\n\"\"\"\n\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\n\nimport pylops\nfrom pylops.utils.metrics import snr\n\n\ndef build_problem(rng):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: return (Op, Reg, y, xtrue or None, noise_std or None).\"\"\"\n    nt = 201\n    t = np.arange(nt) * 0.004\n    h, _, hcenter = pylops.utils.wavelets.ricker(t[:41], f0=15)\n    Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h=h, offset=hcenter)\n    Reg = pylops.SecondDerivative(nt)\n\n    xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 3 * t) * np.exp(-t)  # smooth model\n    noise_std = 0.05\n    y = Op @ xtrue + noise_std * rng.standard_normal(nt)\n    return Op, Reg, y, xtrue, noise_std\n\n\ndef solve(Op, Reg, y, eps, x0):\n    \"\"\"ADAPT: the user's solver call with everything but ``eps`` fixed.\"\"\"\n    Opaug = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * Reg])\n    yaug = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(Reg.shape[0])])\n    return pylops.cgls(Opaug, yaug, x0=x0, niter=200, tol=1e-10)[0]\n\n\ndef lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm):\n    \"\"\"Index of the L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log).\n\n    Points are ordered by increasing eps so that the corner between the\n    \"under-regularised\" (vertical) and \"over-regularised\" (horizontal) branches\n    has positive curvature. A second knee with opposite curvature can appear\n    at very large eps (model pushed into the null space of the regulariser):\n    it is not the L-curve corner.\n    \"\"\"\n    order = np.argsort(epsilons)\n    lr, lx = np.log(rnorm[order]), np.log(xnorm[order])\n    # parametrise by arc length to make finite differences uniform\n    s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(np.hypot(np.diff(lr), np.diff(lx)))])\n    dr, dx = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lx, s)\n    ddr, ddx = np.gradient(dr, s), np.gradient(dx, s)\n    kappa = (dr * ddx - ddr * dx) / (dr**2 + dx**2) ** 1.5\n    kappa[[0, -1]] = -np.inf  # ends are not valid corners\n    kappa_orig = np.empty_like(kappa)\n    kappa_orig[order] = kappa\n    return int(np.argmax(kappa_orig)), kappa_orig\n\n\ndef discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, noise_std, n, tau=1.02):\n    \"\"\"Largest eps whose

Here the problem is also underdetermined (80 data points, 200 unknowns).\n\n**What the other criteria say**\n\n| Criterion | `eps` | Non-zeros | SNR |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Discrepancy (residual ≤ 0.15·√80 = 1.34) | **0.51** | 18 | 11.4 dB |\n| Best SNR (needs the true model) | 0.24 | 43 | 12.3 dB |\n| Your `eps=2.0` | 2.0 | 4–5 | about 3.5 dB |\n\n**Why 0.51**\n- It is the largest `eps` whose residual matches the known noise level, and it needs no knowledge of the true model.\n- It is close to the best-SNR peak, within about 1 dB.\n- At `eps=2.0` the residual is about 2.6, well above the noise level of 1.34. That model is over-regularised: it has too few spikes and a poor SNR.\n\n**Caveats**\n- 0.51 still gives 18 non-zeros against 6 true spikes. The L1 penalty adds small false spikes at the noise level before it recovers the full amplitude of the real ones.\n- If you want about 6 non-zeros, `eps≈1.4` gives exactly 6 (SNR 5.4 dB), but the data are then under-fit. A common fix is to take the support from `fista` at `eps≈0.5–1.4` and re-fit by least squares on that support.\n- I only tested one noise realisation.\n\n**Line to change:** `eps=2.0` → `eps=0.51` in your `fista` call. Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I can't give you an L-curve corner I'd trust for this problem, so I chose `eps ≈ 0.63` with the discrepancy principle instead. `sweep.py` is saved and runs, and its last line is `EPS_CHOSEN=0.631`.

**Why not the corner.** I swept `eps` from 1e-3 to 10 (41 log-spaced values, 2000 FISTA iterations each) and computed the log-log curvature of residual against L1 norm. The largest |curvature| is at `eps ≈ 2.0`, but I don't trust it, for three reasons:
- **Weak peak.** Over `eps` 0.2 to 2.5, |κ| only varies between about 0.85 and 1.28, so there is no sharp elbow. The script's peak-to-median ratio of 4.9 looks larger than it is, because the median is dominated by the nearly flat low-`eps` end. The "NOT reliable" label in the script's printout is hardcoded text, not a computed test.
- **Edge effect.** The maximum sits right where the curve collapses toward x=0, which is where the curvature estimate is least stable.
- **Overfitted to underfit.** At `eps = 2` the residual is 2.56, nearly twice the noise floor of σ·√M = 0.15·√80 = 1.34. The model error there is 2.5, against 0.9 at its best in the sweep. That is your 4-to-5 non-zeros result: too much shrinkage.

**What I chose.** Because you know σ, the discrepancy principle applies: pick `eps` so that ‖Ax−y‖ ≈ σ√M. The script interpolates this on a finer grid and gets `eps ≈ 0.63`, with residual 1.34.

**Caveats:**
- At `eps = 0.63` the solution has about 10 non-zeros, not 6, and the model error is about 1.09 (against 2.5 at `eps = 2`). The extra entries are small spurious ones.
- `eps` between 1.0 and 1.3 gives 6 to 7 non-zeros, but its residual of 1.7 to 1.9 is well above the noise level.
- If you want exactly the true support, pick the support at `eps ≈ 0.63` to 1.0, then refit by least squares on those columns. That also removes the L1 shrinkage bias. I didn't add this to the script.
- FISTA's `eps` is a threshold scale and not exactly the λ in the L1 objective, so only compare values within this setup.

import numpy as np
import pylops

rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
N, M = 200, 80
sigma = 0.15
A = rng.standard_normal((M, N)) / np.sqrt(M)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
xtrue = np.zeros(N)
xtrue[rng.choice(N, 6, replace=False)] = rng.uniform(1, 3, 6) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 6)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(M)

eps_grid = np.logspace(-3, 1, 41)
res, l1, nnz, err = [], [], [], []
for eps in eps_grid:
    x = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(Op, y, eps=eps, niter=2000, show=False)[0]
    res.append(np.linalg.norm(Op @ x - y))
    l1.append(np.abs(x).sum())
    nnz.append(int(np.sum(np.abs(x) > 1e-6)))
    err.append(np.linalg.norm(x - xtrue))
res, l1, nnz, err = map(np.asarray, (res, l1, nnz, err))

# L-curve in log-log space; curvature restricted to the non-degenerate range
ok = (nnz > 0) & (l1 > 1e-3)
t = np.log10(eps_grid[ok])
u, v = np.log10(res[ok]), np.log10(l1[ok])
du, dv = np.gradient(u, t), np.gradient(v, t)
ddu, ddv = np.gradient(du, t), np.gradient(dv, t)
curv = (du * ddv - dv * ddu) / (du**2 + dv**2) ** 1.5
ac = np.abs(curv)
peak = ac.max() / np.median(ac)
eps_corner = eps_grid[ok][np.argmax(ac)]
print(f"L-curve max |curvature| at eps={eps_corner:.4g}, peak/median = {peak:.2f} "
      f"(a real corner would be a sharp peak, >>1) -> NOT reliable")

# Discrepancy principle (noise std known): ||Ax-y|| = sigma*sqrt(M), refined on a fine grid
target = sigma * np.sqrt(M)
fine = np.logspace(np.log10(0.3), np.log10(1.5), 61)
fr, fn, fe = [], [], []
for e in fine:
    x = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(Op, y, eps=e, niter=2000, show=False)[0]
    fr.append(np.linalg.norm(Op @ x - y)); fn.append(int(np.sum(np.abs(x) > 1e-6)))
    fe.append(np.linalg.norm(x - xtrue))
fr = np.asarray(fr)
eps_disc = float(np.exp(np.interp(target, fr, np.log(fine))))  # fr is increasing in eps

print(f"{'eps':>9} {'resid':>8} {'L1':>8} {'nnz':>4} {'|x-xt|':>8}")
for i, e in enumerate(eps_grid):
    print(f"{e:9.4f} {res[i]:8.4f} {l1[i]:8.4f} {nnz[i]:4d} {err[i]:8.4f}")
print(f"noise floor sigma*sqrt(M) = {target:.4f}; true nnz = 6")
j = np.argmin(np.abs(fine - eps_disc))
print(f"discrepancy: eps={eps_disc:.4g} (nearest grid: nnz={fn[j]}, err={fe[j]:.3f})")
print(f"EPS_CHOSEN={eps_disc:.3g}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I recommend **`eps ≈ 0.63`**. This comes from the discrepancy principle, not from an L-curve corner, because this L-curve doesn't have a usable corner.

`sweep.py` sweeps 41 values of `eps` from 1e-3 to 10 (log-spaced) and prints `EPS_CHOSEN=0.631` as its last line. It also saves the curve as `lcurve.png`. I ran it and checked these results.

**Why the corner isn't usable**
- The distance from the chord (the straight line joining the curve's two ends) is almost flat across the middle of the curve: 0.452 at `eps`=0.16, 0.463 at 0.32 and 0.460 at 0.63. No point stands out as the corner.
- The two corner detectors disagree. Max distance from the chord gives `eps`=0.32 (30 non-zeros). Max curvature gives `eps`=0.16 (57 non-zeros). Your true model has 6.
- This is typical for L1 problems, where the curve bends gradually rather than sharply.
- The all-zero solutions at `eps` ≥ 5 have an L1 norm of 0, so the script drops them before taking logs.

**Why 0.63**
- You know the noise level, so I used the discrepancy principle: pick the `eps` where the residual ‖Ax−y‖ matches σ√M = 0.15·√80 ≈ 1.34. At `eps`=0.63 the residual is 1.341.
- That gives 10 non-zeros, which is more than your 6. FISTA's soft-thresholding shrinks the large coefficients, and a few small spurious ones get through.
- The lowest error against `xtrue` is at `eps`≈0.25, but you can't know that on real data.

**Your `eps=2.0`**
- It's over-regularized. The residual is 2.56, about 1.9 times the noise floor, and the model has 4 non-zeros, which is fewer than the true 6.
- Coefficient amplitudes are also biased low. If you want exactly the right support, take `eps`≈0.63 and then re-fit the amplitudes by least squares on the non-zeros. Alternatively, use a larger `eps` of 0.8–1.0 (6–8 non-zeros) and accept more bias.
✓ pass final-value ×0.5

import numpy as np
import pylops

rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
N, M = 200, 80
sigma = 0.15
A = rng.standard_normal((M, N)) / np.sqrt(M)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
xtrue = np.zeros(N)
xtrue[rng.choice(N, 6, replace=False)] = rng.uniform(1, 3, 6) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 6)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(M)

epss = np.logspace(-3, 1, 41)
res, l1, nnz, err = [], [], [], []
for eps in epss:
    x = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(Op, y, eps=eps, niter=2000, show=False)[0]
    res.append(np.linalg.norm(Op @ x - y))
    l1.append(np.abs(x).sum())
    nnz.append(int((np.abs(x) > 1e-6).sum()))
    err.append(np.linalg.norm(x - xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(xtrue))
res, l1, nnz, err = map(np.array, (res, l1, nnz, err))

# Corner: max distance from the chord in normalized log-log space
valid = np.where(l1 > 1e-8)[0]  # drop all-zero models (log undefined)
lx, ly = np.log10(res[valid]), np.log10(l1[valid])
px = (lx - lx.min()) / (lx.max() - lx.min())
py = (ly - ly.min()) / (ly.max() - ly.min())
p0, p1 = np.array([px[0], py[0]]), np.array([px[-1], py[-1]])
d = p1 - p0
dist = np.abs(d[0] * (py - p0[1]) - d[1] * (px - p0[0])) / np.linalg.norm(d)
distf = np.full(len(epss), np.nan); distf[valid] = dist
# Also max curvature (discrete)
dx, dy = np.gradient(px), np.gradient(py)
ddx, ddy = np.gradient(dx), np.gradient(dy)
curv = np.abs(dx * ddy - dy * ddx) / (dx**2 + dy**2) ** 1.5
ic = int(valid[np.argmax(dist)])
ik = int(valid[np.argmax(curv[2:-2]) + 2])

# Discrepancy principle reference: ||r|| ~ sigma*sqrt(M)
target = sigma * np.sqrt(M)
idp = int(np.argmin(np.abs(res - target)))

print(f"{'eps':>9} {'resid':>8} {'L1':>8} {'nnz':>4} {'relerr':>7} {'dist':>6}")
for i in range(len(epss)):
    tag = (" <-chord" if i == ic else "") + (" <-curv" if i == ik else "") + (" <-DP" if i == idp else "")
    print(f"{epss[i]:9.4f} {res[i]:8.3f} {l1[i]:8.3f} {nnz[i]:4d} {err[i]:7.3f} {distf[i]:6.3f}{tag}")
print(f"noise level sigma*sqrt(M) = {target:.3f}")
print(f"chord corner eps={epss[ic]:.4g} (nnz={nnz[ic]}), curvature corner eps={epss[ik]:.4g} (nnz={nnz[ik]}), "
      f"discrepancy eps={epss[idp]:.4g} (nnz={nnz[idp]}), best-err eps={epss[np.argmin(err)]:.4g}")

try:
    import matplotlib
    matplotlib.use("Agg")
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
    fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(5, 4))
    ax.loglog(res, l1, "o-", ms=3)
    ax.plot(res[ic], l1[ic], "rs", label=f"chord corner eps={epss[ic]:.3g}")
    ax.plot(res[idp], l1[idp], "g^", label=f"discrepancy eps={epss[idp]:.3g}")
    ax.set_xlabel("||Ax-y||_2"); ax.set_ylabel("||x||_1"); ax.legend()
    fig.tight_layout(); fig.savefig("lcurve.png", dpi=120)
except Exception as e:
    print("plot skipped:", e)

print("L-curve corner is ambiguous (distance-to-chord is flat, curvature and chord disagree);")
print("falling back to the discrepancy principle using the known noise level.")
print(f"EPS_CHOSEN={epss[idp]:.4g}")

Judge votes✗ ✗ ✗
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I recommend **`eps ≈ 1.2`**. The script prints `EPS_CHOSEN=1.207`, and the corner is a weak one, so treat the number as soft.

**What the script does** (`sweep.py`)
- It runs `fista` (2000 iterations) over 61 log-spaced `eps` values from 0.01 to 4.
- For each value it records the residual ‖Op·x − y‖₂ and the L1 norm of the model. It also records the non-zero count and the error against `xtrue`, which you wouldn't have on real data.
- It takes the corner as the point of maximum curvature of the log-log L-curve.

**An earlier version gave a wrong answer.** My first run reported `eps=6.31`. That was the all-zero solution, where log‖x‖₁ is undefined, so the curvature was meaningless. The script now ignores points with fewer than 2 non-zeros and the grid edges.

**Result**
- Maximum curvature is at `eps ≈ 1.21`: residual 1.87, ‖x‖₁ = 5.0, and **6 non-zeros**, which matches the true sparsity.
- Your `eps=2.0` is past the corner. It over-shrinks (5 non-zeros, residual 2.55), and the error against `xtrue` is 2.5, versus 1.7 at the corner.

**Caveats**
- The corner is shallow. Curvature stays at about 0.46–0.53 across `eps` ≈ 1.1–1.6 (6–7 non-zeros), so anything in that range is about equally justified. I'd treat 1.2 as the centre of that plateau, not a sharp optimum.
- The discrepancy principle (residual ≈ σ√M = 1.34) points lower, at `eps ≈ 0.6`. That gives 11 non-zeros, so it over-fits. L1 shrinkage biases the residual upward, which is why I trust the L-curve more here.
- The error against `xtrue` is smallest at `eps ≈ 0.25`, but that solution has about 40 non-zeros. If you care about support recovery, the corner is the better choice. If you only care about amplitude error, you could refit the selected support by least squares to remove the shrinkage bias.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I recommend **`eps ≈ 1.2`**. The script prints `EPS_CHOSEN=1.207`, and the corner is a weak one, so treat the number as soft.

**What the script does** (`sweep.py`)
- It runs `fista` (2000 iterations) over 61 log-spaced `eps` values from 0.01 to 4.
- For each value it records the residual ‖Op·x − y‖₂ and the L1 norm of the model. It also records the non-zero count and the error against `xtrue`, which you wouldn't have on real data.
- It takes the corner as the point of maximum curvature of the log-log L-curve.

**An earlier version gave a wrong answer.** My first run reported `eps=6.31`. That was the all-zero solution, where log‖x‖₁ is undefined, so the curvature was meaningless. The script now ignores points with fewer than 2 non-zeros and the grid edges.

**Result**
- Maximum curvature is at `eps ≈ 1.21`: residual 1.87, ‖x‖₁ = 5.0, and **6 non-zeros**, which matches the true sparsity.
- Your `eps=2.0` is past the corner. It over-shrinks (5 non-zeros, residual 2.55), and the error against `xtrue` is 2.5, versus 1.7 at the corner.

**Caveats**
- The corner is shallow. Curvature stays at about 0.46–0.53 across `eps` ≈ 1.1–1.6 (6–7 non-zeros), so anything in that range is about equally justified. I'd treat 1.2 as the centre of that plateau, not a sharp optimum.
- The discrepancy principle (residual ≈ σ√M = 1.34) points lower, at `eps ≈ 0.6`. That gives 11 non-zeros, so it over-fits. L1 shrinkage biases the residual upward, which is why I trust the L-curve more here.
- The error against `xtrue` is smallest at `eps ≈ 0.25`, but that solution has about 40 non-zeros. If you care about support recovery, the corner is the better choice. If you only care about amplitude error, you could refit the selected support by least squares to remove the shrinkage bias.

import numpy as np
import pylops

rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
N, M = 200, 80
A = rng.standard_normal((M, N)) / np.sqrt(M)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
xtrue = np.zeros(N)
xtrue[rng.choice(N, 6, replace=False)] = rng.uniform(1, 3, 6) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 6)
sigma = 0.15
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(M)

epss = np.logspace(-2, np.log10(4), 61)
res, l1, nnz, err = [], [], [], []
for e in epss:
    x = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(Op, y, eps=e, niter=2000, show=False)[0]
    res.append(np.linalg.norm(Op @ x - y)); l1.append(np.abs(x).sum())
    nnz.append(int(np.sum(np.abs(x) > 1e-6))); err.append(np.linalg.norm(x - xtrue))
res, l1 = np.array(res), np.array(l1)

# corner: max curvature of the log-log L-curve (parametrised by log eps)
t = np.log(epss); u, v = np.log(res), np.log(l1 + 1e-12)
du, dv = np.gradient(u, t), np.gradient(v, t)
d2u, d2v = np.gradient(du, t), np.gradient(dv, t)
with np.errstate(all='ignore'):
    kappa = (du * d2v - dv * d2u) / (du**2 + dv**2) ** 1.5
# exclude the trivial all-zero end (log L1 -> -inf) and the grid edges
valid = np.isfinite(kappa) & (np.array(nnz) >= 2)
valid[:2] = False; valid[-2:] = False
ic = int(np.argmax(np.where(valid, np.abs(kappa), -np.inf)))

# discrepancy principle for reference: ||r|| ~ sigma*sqrt(M)
target = sigma * np.sqrt(M)
idp = int(np.argmin(np.abs(res - target)))

print(f"{'eps':>9} {'resid':>8} {'L1':>8} {'nnz':>4} {'|x-xt|':>7} {'curv':>8}")
for i, e in enumerate(epss):
    m = " <corner" if i == ic else (" <discrep" if i == idp else "")
    print(f"{e:9.4f} {res[i]:8.4f} {l1[i]:8.4f} {nnz[i]:4d} {err[i]:7.3f} {kappa[i]:8.2f}{m}")
print(f"noise level sigma*sqrt(M) = {target:.3f}; true nnz=6, true L1={np.abs(xtrue).sum():.3f}")
print(f"corner eps={epss[ic]:.4g} nnz={nnz[ic]}; discrepancy eps={epss[idp]:.4g} nnz={nnz[idp]}")
print(f"EPS_CHOSEN={epss[ic]:.4g}")

import numpy as np
import pylops

rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
N, M = 200, 80
A = rng.standard_normal((M, N)) / np.sqrt(M)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
xtrue = np.zeros(N)
xtrue[rng.choice(N, 6, replace=False)] = rng.uniform(1, 3, 6) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 6)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.15 * rng.standard_normal(M)

xinv = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(Op, y, eps=2.0, niter=500)[0]
